南亞科(2408) 
南以抗拒 亞倒群雄 科不容緩 
投資評等 目標價 
買進 $800 
前次投資建議：2026.07.13： 
「買進」 ，目標價: $710

報告日期：2026.10.13 研 究 員：王穎

摘 要 及 調 整 原 因 
➢ 看好記憶體需求緊俏有利報價走升，維持「買進」 投資評等，目標價 800 元(6 x 27(F)PER)，
潛在上漲空間 51.8%。

基本資料 結論與建議 
2026/10/12 收盤價： 527.00 ➢ 26Q3 營收 1336.5 億元新台幣， QoQ+61.9%，YoY+ 
611.7%，毛利率 85.7% ，營業費用 75.88 億元，營業利
益為 1068.9億元 ， 業外收入24.38億 ， 稅後淨利為 876.3
億元﹐EPS 為 25.4 元，每股淨值為 118.99元。營收成
長及毛利雙雙成長受惠 平均銷售單價季增超過 六十位數
百分比，而季減中個位數百分比，匯率有利低個位數百分
比。 
➢ 展望 26Q4，受惠 AI 需求續強預期 DRAM 將持續缺貨，
且南亞科 AI相關產品已佔營收逾 20%，雖受限產能滿載
出貨量將無法再提升，然而預期合約價將持續於 26Q4上
漲 15~20%，營收及毛利率將持續創高。因此兆豐預估營
收 1556.04 億元，QoQ+16.4%，YoY+417.1%，毛利率 
88.1%，EPS為 30.35 元。 
➢ 展望 2026~2027 年，受惠 AI 需求持續強勁三大原廠於
DRAM產能持續滿載，預期 DDR5、DDR4、DDR3等產
品將持續供不應求。1C 產品預期於 27Q1 開始驗證，有
利打入 AI伺服器供應鏈，預期 AI 占比將逐季提高。因此
兆豐預估營收 4208.9/6993.1 億元，YoY+532.1/66.2%，
毛利率 83.3/86.3%，EPS 為 77.85/133.2 元。南亞科目
前位於(3.9 X 27(F) PER)，看好記憶體需求緊俏報價持續
走升， 維持「買進」投資評等，目標價  800 元(6 x 
27(F)PER)，潛在上漲空間 51.8%。

營 運 分 析

南亞科技為全球前四大 DRAM廠商 
南亞科技主要致力 DRAM (動態隨機存取記憶體)的研發、設計、製造與銷售，
開發自主技術，發展智慧財產權，培育關鍵技術研發團隊，落實綠色科技及
智慧製造全面提升製程效能與產能，是全球第4大的 DRAM公司。公司已成
功開發 10 奈米級 DRAM 製程技術，將可微縮至少 3 個世代，並用以發展
DDR5及 LPDDR5 等下世代產品，強化公司自主技術開發與製程轉換佈局。
第二世代 10 奈米級製程技術(1B)產品 16Gb DDR5 已完成設計與試產，於
2024 年量產。南亞科技具備完整 DRAM 產品線，可因應智慧世代的發展，
滿足多元化的產品需求。預期 1C產品於 27Q1 開始驗證。

- 3 - 
個股報告 
26Q3 ASP成長帶動營收毛利雙雙上升 
26Q3 營收  1336.5 億元新台幣， QoQ+61.9%，YoY+ 611.7%，毛利率
85.7% ，營業費用 75.88 億元，營業利益為 1068.9 億元，業外收入 24.38
億，稅後淨利為 876.3 億元﹐EPS 為 25.4 元，每股淨值為 118.99元。營
收成長及毛利雙雙成長受惠平均銷售單價季增超過六十位數百分比，而季減
中個位數百分比，匯率有利低個位數百分比。

26Q4合約價持續上漲  
展望 26Q4，受惠 AI 需求續強預期 DRAM 將持續缺貨，且南亞科 AI 相關產
品已佔營收逾 20%，雖受限產能滿載出貨量將無法再提升，然而預期合約價
將持續於 26Q4 上漲 15~20%，營收及毛利率將持續創高。因此兆豐預估營
收 1556.04 億元，QoQ+16.4%，YoY+417.1%，毛利率 88.1%，EPS 為 30.35
元。 
圖二、26Q4 預期記憶體報價持續走升

資料來源：南亞科；兆豐投顧預估

季增超過六十位數百
分比。 
26Q4 AI 需求強勁且

續升高。

圖三、DRAM合約價報價走勢圖

資料來源：兆豐投顧整理

南亞科完成 787.2 億元私募案，攜手四大客戶搶佔 AI 供應鏈先機 
南亞科日前完成總額新台幣  787.2 億元的私募認購，吸引四大核心客戶參
與。增資後，相關投資方持股比例達 10.19%。雙方同時簽署供應協議，確
保未來能穩定供應多元 DRAM 產品組合。主要目的在於強化南亞科與客戶
的戰略合作，透過 e-SSD 與網通產品等領域，建立 AI 及雲端服務供應商
（CSP）供應鏈中的雙贏局面。私募資金將注資新廠以提升產能，進一步確
立南亞科在 AI 價值鏈中的市場地位。本次私募後，南亞科總發行股數將達 
34.5 億股。

記憶體供不應求，產能持續擴充 
目前產能為 6.5 萬片/月，產能利用率維持滿載，2026Q1~Q3 資本支出 276
億，2026 以不超過 697 億元為上限，設備佔比五成．5A新廠第一階段 35,900
片/月 於 2026~2029 資本支出約 3,466 億．新廠預計於 2027 年初開始裝機，
主要生產 1B/1C/1D製程，預期 27年底開出 20k/月，2028 年達每月投片量
達 30k/月，主要因應網通、eSSD等產品之強勁需求。

AI 帶動記憶體強勁需求，有利報價持續走升 
展望 2026~2027 年，受惠 AI 需求持續強勁三大原廠於 DRAM 產能持續滿
載，預期DDR5、DDR4、DDR3等產品將持續供不應求。1C產品預期於 27Q1
開始驗證，有利打入 AI 伺服器供應鏈，預期 AI 占比將逐季提高。因此兆豐
預估營收 4208.9/6993.1 億元，YoY+532.1/66.2%，毛利率83.3/86.3%，EPS
為 77.85/133.2 元。

記憶體持續供不應
求，報價持續走升。 
2027 新產能主要 用
於生產 1B/1C/1D 製
程。 
大型私募案確保未來
穩定供應多元 DRAM
產品組合。

評等分級：買進（預期未來六個月股價漲幅超過+30%）; 逢低買進（預期未來六個月股價漲幅介於+15%與+30%之間）; 區間操
作（預期未來六個月股價漲跌幅介於-15%與+15%之間）; 逢高賣出（預期未來六個月股價跌幅介於-15%與-30%之間）；賣出（預
期未來六個月股價跌幅超過-30%）。

水冷機櫃與機殼本業同步走升 
 2Q26 本業穩健成長 ，EPS 1.41 元：晟銘電 2Q26 營收 32.19 億元，
年增 22.5%，毛利率則從前期 21.1%降至 20.5%，主要是因客戶
Storage 相關產品出貨增加，然 Storage 產品已進入成熟階段，客戶
持續每季議價，2Q26 稅後淨利為 2.9 億元，EPS 1.41 元。 
 預期 3Q26 為營運谷底，4Q26 開始爬升：展望 26H2，3Q26 營收年
增 16%，估計 3Q26 稅後 EPS 1.49 元。4Q 通常為傳統旺季，加上
VR200 也會在 4Q26 陸續加入量產，而晟銘電也有參與其中，出貨
量將逐步上升 ， 隨著 VR200 及 ASIC 等新平台陸續進入放量階段，
預期機殼與 Sidecar 出貨量將同步提升。此外，2026 年 ODM 專案
有 8 至 9 成聚焦機櫃業務，涵蓋 OCP、EIA 以及 MGX，預期 L11
系統整合業務營收將進一步提升。 
 維持買進，目標價 140 元：投顧認為晟銘電 4Q26 起受惠 VR200 機
殼與 ASIC 放量，2027 年營運可望進一步成長，且公司已成功切入
Meta 供應鏈，並在冷卻機櫃與機構件取得關鍵認證，可直接承接
Meta 釋出的巨額硬體訂單，且在液冷架構成為主流帶動下，晟銘電
水冷相關業務比重持續提升 ， 使2026 年整體毛利率有望升到 20%以
上 ， 投顧預估2026 年 EPS 6.13 元，2027 年 EPS 9.4 元 ， 維持買進 ，
目標價 140 元。

個股報告

晟銘電 為伺服器機殼大廠，目前
切入 IT 機櫃、水冷機櫃、水冷搭
配的分歧管 
 晟銘電子科技股份有限公司成立於 1976 年 6 月 ， 為台灣伺服器機殼OEM/ODM
製造商， 公司主要銷售電腦機殼及伺服器機殼 ，其用途為桌上型電腦及伺服器等
各項組件之安裝防護設備 ， 近年來公司積極跨足散熱領域並研發設計出液冷散熱
的浸沒式散熱產品。於 2024 年公司推出多款液冷機櫃及液冷散熱系統。晟銘電
提供全球客戶迅速且彈性的 OEM 及 ODM 生產服務。2026 年 1~8 月主要產品
營收占比，伺服器 73%、L11 系統整合 18%、Tooling 6%、DT 3%。

Exhibit 3: 產品組合

Sources : Company、MasterLink

2Q26 本業穩健成長，EPS 1.41
元 
 晟銘電 2Q26 營收 32.19 億元，年增 22.5%，毛利率則從前期 21.1%降至
20.5%，主要是因客戶 Storage 相關產品出貨增加，然 Storage 產品已進入成熟
階段，客戶持續每季議價，2Q26 稅後淨利為 2.9 億元，EPS 1.41 元。

預期 3Q26 為營運谷底，4Q26
開始爬升 
 展望 26H2，3Q26 營收年增 16%，估計 3Q26 稅後 EPS 1.49 元。4Q 通常為
傳統旺季，加上 VR200 也會在 4Q26 陸續加入量產，而晟銘電也有參與其中，
出貨量將逐步上升，隨著 VR200 及 ASIC 等新平台陸續進入放量階段，預期
機殼與 Sidecar 出貨量將同步提升。此外，2026 年 ODM 專案有 8 至 9 成聚焦
機櫃業務，涵蓋 OCP、EIA 以及 MGX，預期 L11 系統整合業務營收將進一步
提升。

今年前八月 L11 系統組裝營收占比已達 18％ ， 而明年L11 系統組裝營收貢獻還
會持續提升。公司在機櫃產品線已相當完整，還可提供配套的水冷配件，機箱
基本上是跟隨著輝達的腳步走 ， 包括GB300、VR200 等標準品機箱都已經開好
模具。海外產能布局，公司目前生產基地仍以中國大陸為主，其中東莞為主要
生產基地，另於寧波設有產能，產品以機殼、機櫃為主，東莞廠自動烤漆線預
計今年底前完成，一天可以完成60 個機櫃烤漆；台灣中壢則主要負責打樣及水
冷產品研發，部分零組件仍由中國生產後運送至中壢進行後段組裝。泰國一期
廠已完成建置並具備生產條件 ， 二期則持續進行設備調校 ， 主要導入烤漆產線，
預計 4Q26 開始試產，後續待客戶完成稽核及認證後逐步量產。泰國二期的烤

漆設備規格除可支援機殼外，亦可延伸至機櫃產品，有助未來將部分機櫃製程
移往海外。今年中壢廠、泰國廠都有新產能加入，而明年資本支出大致也會維
持在大約 7～8 億元。

維持買進，目標價 140 元 
 投顧認為晟銘電 4Q26 起受惠 VR200 機殼與 ASIC 放量，2027 年營運可望進
一步成長 ， 且公司已成功切入Meta 供應鏈 ， 並在冷卻機櫃與機構件取得關鍵認
證，可直接承接Meta 釋出的巨額硬體訂單，且在液冷架構成為主流帶動下，晟
銘電水冷相關業務比重持續提升，使 2026 年整體毛利率有望升到 20%以上，
投顧預估 2026 年 EPS 6. 13 元，2027 年 EPS 9.4 元，維持買進，目標價 140
元。(15x2027EPS)

台新證券投資顧問股份有限公司 台北市仁愛路四段 118 號 16 樓(02)5589-9558  (113) 金管投顧新字第 021 號 
公司拜訪快報

曜越(3540)：2027 年 Gaming PC 與 AI 工 
建議：評等買進

一、曜越為電競電腦零組件大廠，積極切入AI 高效能工
作站系統 
曜越科技股份有限公司成立於 1999 年 11 月，為電腦機
殼、散熱器(模組)及電源供應器製造商，以自有品牌
Thermaltake 銷售桌上型電腦散熱模組、機殼、電源供
應器，專攻 clone 遊戲玩家市場，近年來公司積極整合
機殼、散熱、電源與系統配置能力，從單一產品銷售延
伸至完整 Gaming PC 系統解決方案，滿足高階 
Gaming PC 的效能與穩定性需求。4Q26 公司將切入
AI 工作站(AI Workstation)新業務 ，拓展地端 AI 運算
市場。2026 年 1~9 月主要產品營收占比，Gaming PC 
35%、電源供應器 33%、機殼 14%、散熱 13%、其他
5%。

二、2Q26 本業疲弱，EPS 0.01 元 
曜越 2Q26 營收 10.16 億元，年減 20.5%，毛利率則從
前期 28.1%升至 30.7%，主要是因記憶體漲價，使毛利
率較佳的客製化裝機業務佔比提升， 2Q26 稅後淨利
96.8 萬元，EPS 0.01 元。 
三、曜越從 PC 零組件品牌轉型為系統整合品牌，並積
極切入 AI 工作站市場  
展望下半年 ，3Q26 營收年減 2.9%， 估計3Q26 稅後 EPS 
0.09 元。4Q 通常為傳統旺季，加上 AI 工作站從雲端到
地端的基本條件已於 2026 年第三季成熟，產品規劃完
成，第四季開始進入市場，出貨量將逐步上升。 
2026 年前九月 Gaming PC 營收佔比已達 35%， 超越電
源供應器的 33%，成為最大產品線，象徵系統整合業務
已成為營收核心。曜越的電競 PC 分為三個價格層次：
入門級 1,599-1,799 美元、進階級 2,599 美元、高階
4,399-4,499 美元。其中核心零組件如機殼、散熱、電源
皆是自製，而主機板、CPU、GPU、SSD 等為外購，透
過美國、德國、澳洲、中國等海外子公司進行在地組裝
與銷售。展望下一階段，曜越將複製電競 PC 的成功模
式，推出五款 AI 工作站與 AI PC 產品線，從入門級 AI
電競 PC（View 380 XL，1,801 TOPS）到企業旗艦雙
系統 AI 工作站（A1500 Dual，16,000 TOPS） ，搭載
NVIDIA RTX 5090、RTX PRO 6000 Blackwell 及 AMD 
Radeon AI PRO R9700 等高階 GPU。AI 工作站從雲端
到地端的基本條件已於 2026 年第三季成熟，產品規劃
完成，第四季開始進入市場，2027 年營收貢獻將逐季顯
現 ， 目標2027 年占整體業績一成 ， 主要鎖定AI 開發者、
專業創作者、學術科研及中小企業等應用。這代表曜越
正式從傳統電競跨足企業級 AI 運算、專業開發與數位
創作的地端商機，由於商用工作站毛利通常優於一般消
費級產品，這將是明年拉升獲利結構與擴展新市場的最
大看點。 
四、評等買進理由 
投顧認為曜越已從機箱、電源供應器及散熱等 PC 零組
件，轉型至拓展更高毛利率的電競PC 系統整合與 AI 工
作站業務，使 2026 年整體毛利率有望升到 30%以上，
預期 2027 年營收將成長 15%，估計 2026 年 EPS 0.46
元，2027 年 EPS 1.95 元，給予買進的建議，目標價31
元。(16x2027EPS)。

耕興 (6146 TT)  Buy

耕興評價已修正，有機會受惠轉單效應，給 Buy 的投資建
議。  
投資建議 ：美國 FCC 有機率禁止，銷往美國的電子產品在中國
實驗室進行測試，耕興有望接受大量轉單，給 Buy 的投資建議。  
公司簡介： 耕興主要從事電子產品之電磁相容 (EMC) 認證、安規
檢測服務、無線網通認證、手機認證、數位電視認證及防磁零
組件之銷售，全球知名的手機、平板電腦與 NB 業者、晶片廠、
網通廠等多為耕興的客戶。  
耕興的 5G 產品、高階網通的檢測市佔率全球第一： FCC 公布
1H26 的 5G 產品、高階網通檢測的耕興市佔率，分別為 23.97% 、
47.3% ，皆為全球第一。  
FCC 將投票決定中國實驗室未來的商機 ： 路透社 (Reuters) 報導 ，
FCC 將於 10/29/2026 投票表決，來決定賣到美國的電子產品，
是否要禁止在中國實驗室 (非 MRA 國家 )進行測試 ， 若表決通過 ，
預計將於 12/2028 開始生效。  
若 FCC 表決通過，對耕興有利： 耕興具備 FCC 的 MRA 資格，
若 FCC 禁止中國實驗室 ， 將會有大量相關訂單轉移至台灣廠商 ，
耕興檢測產能為全球第一，將直接受惠轉單效應。  
群益預估耕興 2026 年 /2027 年稅後 EPS 12.44 元 /13.73 元： 群
益預估耕興 2026 年 /2027 年營收為 49.35 億元 /53.89 億元，
YoY+9.80%/+9.19% ；稅後純益 12.68 億元 /13.99 億元，

個股報告

2 群益投顧 
  ESG ：  
Environmental ：與 2021 年相比， 2022 年之用電度數較 2021 年減少 0.68% 、
減紙 42%( 減碳 7.92 公噸 CO 2 )。未來規劃：華亞實驗室建置太陽能發電系
統；建置雨水或空調冷凝水回收系統；分階段換發 LED 節能燈具、省電空
調；並於馬達設備增設變頻器以節省用電。  
Social ：體恤員工與資助社團法人嘉義縣扶緣服務協會、新港文教基金會、
與新港奉天宮之金虎爺慢跑活動。國內交易量一百萬元以上的供應商中，
92% 已簽屬「供應商社會責任承諾書」並定期查核；零件部跟生產相關的原
料供應商皆已簽署「不使用衝突礦產聲明書」。  
Governance ：董事會治理制度包含「董事會議事規範」、衡量績效目標落實
之「董事績效評估辦法」、確保誠信經營之「誠信經營守則」、避免貪腐與
符合公平交易法之「誠信經營作業程序及行為指南」等。訂定「資訊安全政
策規範」並以 PDCA (Plan, Do, Check, Act) 循環方式管理。  
公司簡介：  
耕興成立於 1997 年，主要從事電子產品之電磁相容 (EMC) 認證、安規檢測
服務、無線網通認證、手機認證、數位電視認證及防磁零組件之銷售，全球
知名的手機、平板電腦與 NB 業者、晶片廠、網通廠等多為耕興的客戶。  
由於耕興具備 GCF 、 PTCRB 組織的符合性檢測認證，以及加拿大與日本發
證權，只要客戶通過公司認證後即可銷往多國，可降低檢驗所需時間與成
本。  
根據 2022 年 FCC 所公布的資料，耕興在 5G/Wi-Fi 的檢測市占率已經是世
界第一，產能也是全球第一。手機 /網通賣的款式越多，對耕興越有利，單
一產品的出貨量對耕興營收無關。  
台灣無線通訊檢測相關同業包含東研信超 (6840 TT) 、穩得 (6761 TT) 和敦吉
(2459 TT) ，耕興的策略是不斷地去投入比同業多的 CAPEX ，做最先進規格
的檢測，像是毫米波、 Wi-Fi 7 的檢測，以提升營收與獲利。  
營收占比 _地區別：  
浩興為耕興的中國子公司。

資料來源：耕興法說簡報。

### Page 3

個股報告  
                                        2026 年 10 月 14 日

3 群益投顧 
AI 筆電、企業級 AP 檢測需求帶動耕興 1H26 營運成長：  
耕興表示， 1H26 獲利增長主要來自於實體 AI 終端裝置發展同時結合非
MRA(Mutual Recognition Arrangement ，相互承認協議 ) 國家地區 FCC 
PAG(Pre-Approval Guidance) 轉單效應帶動大量高階機種檢測需求。  
耕興內部統計最顯著增長的兩大產業族群來自於 AI 筆電及北美企業級 AP
檢測訂單需求增加。  
2H26 營運有望逐季成長：  
展望 2H26 同樣伴隨 PAG 轉單效應持續消化大量湧入的 AI 筆電及北美企業
級 AP 檢測訂單。檢測需求帶動下， 3Q26 獲利表現將有機會比 2Q26 強勁，
同時 4Q26 的獲利也有機會更上一層樓， 4Q26 是 2027 年高階旗艦級手機種
換機潮的檢測高峰期。  
耕興的 5G 產品、高階網通的檢測市佔率全球第一：  
FCC 公布 1H26 的 5G 產品、高階網通檢測的耕興市佔率，分別為 23.97% 、
47.3% ，皆為全球第一。  
FCC 將投票決定中國實驗室未來的商機：  
路透社 (Reuters) 報導， FCC 將於 10/29/2026 投票表決，來決定賣到美國的電
子產品，是否要禁止在中國實驗室 (非 MRA 國家 )進行測試，若表決通過，
預計將於 12/2028 開始生效。  
FCC 主席表示， 2025 年賣到美國的電子產品，約有 82% 是在中國實驗室進
行測試，而在美國實驗室測試的比例不到 4%。  
若 FCC 表決通過，對耕興有利：  
耕興具備 FCC 的 MRA 資格，若 FCC 禁止中國實驗室，將會有大量相關訂
單轉移至台灣廠商，耕興檢測產能為全球第一，將直接受惠轉單效應。

7 群益投顧 
  【投資評等說明】  
  評等  定義  
  強力買進 (Strong Buy)  首次評等潛在上漲空間 ≥ 35%  
  買進 (Buy)  15% ≤首次評等潛在上漲空間 <35%  
  區間操作 (Trading Buy) 5% ≤首次評等潛在上漲空間 <15%

中立 (Neutral)  
無法由基本面給予投資評等  
預期近期股價將處於盤整  
建議降低持股

【投資評等調整機制】  
  投資評等係「首次」給予特定個股投資評等時，其前一日收盤價相較 3 個月
目標價之潛在上漲空間計算而得。個股投資評等分為四個等級，定義如上。
爾後的投資評等係依循「首次評等」，直到停止推薦。   
「強力買進」、「買進」及「區間操作」均有 upside 目標價。差別僅在於，
首次評等時潛在報酬率不同。「中立」則無目標價。  
一旦我們給予特定個股「強力買進」 、 「買進」或「區間操作」之投資評等，
就是責任的開始，爾後將透過各式研究報告作定期性、持續性追蹤基本面及
股價變化，直到停止推薦。  
停止推薦情境：  
1.  達目標價。  
2.  雖未達原訂目標價，但檢視基本面、訊息面、籌碼面等多方訊息，研
判股價上漲空間已然有限，將適時出具降評報告。  
3.  推薦後股價不漲反跌。

【免責聲明】  
  本研究報告僅提供予特定人之客戶作為參考資料「非經同意不得轉載」 。我
們並不確保此資訊的完整性與正確性，投資人應了解，報告中有關未來預測
之陳述可能不會實現，因而不應被依賴。而且此報告並非根據特定投資目的
或依預定對象之財務狀況所撰寫出來的，因此，此研究報告的目的，既非對
投資人於買賣證券、選擇權、期貨或其他證券相關之衍生性商品提供詢價服
務，亦非作為進行交易的要約。投資人應注意到相關證券之價值及收益，可
能會有無預警地上升或下降，產生投資回報金額可能比原始投資來得少的
情形。

 절대 (달러환산) 0.3  (11.5) 239.5

3Q26 전사 예상 영업이익 100조원 
DS부문 예상 영업이익은 100.0조원(OPM 67.4%, YoY +1538.5%, QoQ +14.5%) 으로, 
달러환율 1,380원기준, 메모리와 비메모리 사업부문 예상 영업손익은 각각 102.0조원,    
-2.0조원으로 추정한다. 1) 메모리반도체 가격 상승세가 이어지는 가운데, HBM4 판매 
본격화에 따른 영향을 고려하면 DRAM/NAND 분기 평균 가격 상승폭은 각각 +18.0%, 
+16.0%으로 추정하고, 2)비메모리반도체 사업 적자는 지속될 전망이기 때문이다. 다만 
특별 성과급 지급에 따른 충당금 영향을 제외하고 선단공정 중심의 파운드리 가동률을 
고려하면 적자폭은 유의미하게 감소한 것으로 파악된다는 점이 긍정적이다.   
SDC부문 예상 영업이익은 1.1조원(OPM 13.0%, YoY -9.6%, QoQ +54.9%)으로, 1)국내 
주력 고객사향 폴더블 스마트폰 판매 호조가 사업부의 외형 성장에 긍정적으로 작용했을 
것으로 추정됨에도 불구하고, 2) 8.6세대 IT OLED 패널 양산 개시에 따른 감가상각비(약 
0.3조원 수준)와 북미 고객 중심의 판가 인하 압박 영향으로 전년동기대비 수익성은 약 
2%pt 훼손이 불가피할 전망이다. DX부문 예상 영업손익은 -1.1조원(YoY 적자전환, QoQ 
적자지속)으로, 메모리반도체를 포함한 IT 부품 가격 부담이 전분기대비 확대되고 있기 
때문이다. MX와 VD/DA사업부 예상 손익은 각각 -0.9조원, -0.2조원으로 추정한다.

투자의견 BUY, 목표주가 63만원으로 상향 조정  
메모리반도체의 가격 상승폭이 다소 둔화되겠지만, 2027~2028년에도 타이트한 수급 
환경이 이어질 전망이라는 점을 주목한다. 1)시장은 신규 증설과 HBM 관련 노이즈로 인한 
2028년 피크아웃 가능성을 우려하고 있으나, HBM 의 범용 DRAM 생산능력 잠식과 AI 
추론 확산에 따른 고용량 메모리 수요를 감안하면 공급 제약의 해소 시점은 예상보다 
늦어질 가능성이 높기 때문이다. 뿐만 아니라, 2)메모리 업사이클이 장기화될 경우 동사 
FCF는 과거대비 구조적으로 확대되어 보다 친화적인 주주환원 정책(2027~2029년, 
2027년 1월 예정)을 제시할 가능성도 배제할 수 없기 때문이다. 동사 2027년 연간 실적 
추정치 상향 조정에 따라 목표주가를 63만원(2027년 BPS * Target PBR 3.5배 적용)으로 
상향 조정한다. 피크아웃 우려에 따른 주가 조정은 중장기 관점에서 매수 기회다.

2026 年三季度以来，全球市场巨震：
股市⽅⾯， AI 科技股遭遇年内第⼆轮剧烈调整。 10 ⽉初 OpenAI 披露年化收⼊接近 500 亿美
元，远低于市场预期的 700 亿美元，引发 AI 概念股⼤规模抛售。费城半导体指数连跌 8 个交易
⽇，创 2008 年以来最⻓连跌纪录；纳指单周累计下跌 4.48% ，为 6 ⽉以来最⼤单周跌幅；英伟
达 9 个交易⽇市值较⾼点蒸发约 0.6 万亿美元。韩国股市由上半年全球最强市场转为重灾区，
KOSPI 指数 10 ⽉ 6 ⽇以来连跌 5 ⽇、累计跌幅近 15% ，创 8 ⽉以来新低。A 股科技成⻓显著回
调，创业板指从 6 ⽉ 9 ⽇的 3961 点回落⾄ 10 ⽉中旬的 2494 点，跌幅接近 40% ，上证指数失守
3700 点，⽩酒、银⾏等红利消费板块逆势⾛强。港股恒⽣科技指数年内跌幅仍超 25% ，点位
接近 2024 年 “9·24” ⾏情区间。
债市⽅⾯，美债收益率全线飙升。美联储 9 ⽉ 16 ⽇宣布加息 25 个基点⾄ 3.75%-4.00% ，为

2023 年 7 ⽉以来⾸次加息，点阵图显⽰年内仍有⼀次加息。 10 年期美债收益率从 7 ⽉中旬的
4.55% 左右⼀路上⾏， 10 ⽉ 7 ⽇升⾄ 5.365% ，创 2002 年 4 ⽉以来新⾼； 30 年期美债收益率升
⾄ 5.732% 。中债则延续低位震荡， 10 年期国债收益率在 1.68% 附近，中美 10 年期利差倒挂超
过 350 个基点，处于历史⾼位区间。
⼤宗商品⽅⾯，中东冲突再度升级，美伊在霍尔⽊兹海峡附近冲突加剧，胡塞武装袭击沙特
能源设施，油价重回 100 美元 / 桶上⽅， 10 ⽉中旬 WTI 原油单周再涨超 10% 。贵⾦属则⾼位震
荡：⾦价⾃ 2026 年 1 ⽉创历史新⾼后最⼤回撤约 25% ， 7 ⽉⼀度回落⾄ 4000 美元 / 盎司附近， 8
⽉反弹⾄ 4600 美元 / 盎司， 10 ⽉以来⼜回落⾄ 4100 ⾄ 4200 美元 / 盎司区间，年内由涨转跌。
美债、美股、⻩⾦、 A 股的定价逻辑都在剧烈重构，踏准节奏的投资者有效规避了回撤，追⾼热

⻔板块的则损失惨重。为了帮⼤家看清 2026 年四季度⼤类资产轮动与⻛向变化，我们邀请了百
亿私募敦和资管⾸席经济学家徐⼩庆，于 10 ⽉ 25 ⽇周⽇晚 19:00 担任 Alpha 线上闭⻔私享会主讲
嘉宾，分享他对四季度宏观⼤类资产配置⻛向的最新研判。

### Page 2

10 ⽉ 25 ⽇周⽇晚上 19 点，百亿私募基⾦敦和资管的⾸席经济学家徐⼩庆主讲的Alpha 线上闭⻔私
享会，将会分享他最近研究关于股票、债券、商品、外汇、⻩⾦等⼤类资产的最新变化，研判
当前宏观⾛势与 2026 下半资产配置⽅向，看清背后的配置逻辑与关键时机，助⼒个⼈的关键决
策。
这是徐⼩庆今年第三次做客 Alpha 闭⻔会。
2 ⽉的分享：在⻩⾦⽩银暴跌后，他提出贵⾦属将从单边上涨进⼊震荡格局，并提⽰美股居⺠
参与度已超互联⽹泡沫时期、市场脆弱性显著上升。
7 ⽉的分享：他将半导体与年初的⽩银⾛势类⽐，提⽰ AI 科技股可能正在构筑阶段性顶部，同
时判断⻩⾦进⼊⾼位震荡、 4000 美元 / 盎司以下具备配置价值。
此后数⽉的市场演绎印证了上述多项判断。站在四季度的新起点，美债利率为何突破 5% ⼤幅上
⾏，何时迎来拐点？ A 股⻛格切换是否已经开启？⻩⾦何时王者归来？这些议题都将在本次分享
中得到详细展开。
徐⼩庆曾任中⾦公司⾸席债券分析师、董事总经理，带队 4 次当选新财富固定收益研究 “ 最佳分

析师 ” 第⼀名，连续 10 年⼊围前三名。在⼗年间持续获得掌管数万亿资产的⾦融机构集体投票⽀
持，徐⼩庆是中国 “ 最懂利率 “ 的⼏个⼈之⼀。2013 年，徐⼩庆加⼊百亿私募基⾦敦和资管担任
⾸席经济学家、宏观策略总监，并担任敦和宏观对冲系列基⾦的投资经理。
20 多年的从业经历⾥，从卖⽅研究最佳分析师到百亿私募的买⽅投资⼈，徐⼩庆将对中国利率
变动影响资产轮动的前沿研究，⽤于投资实践指引资产配置。早在 2018 年，华尔街⻅闻就邀请
徐⼩庆打造⻅闻⼤师课《⼗年利率⼼法》。在课程中，徐⼩庆⽴⾜中国市场，总结出⼀套⾏之有
效的中国特⾊ “ 利率为锚 ” 的资产轮动与配置⽅法。这是他从业 20 多年间，亲历中国债券市场 5 轮
⽜熊轮动，对中国利率周期影响资产轮动进⾏系统跟踪研究的成果。这⻔课程⽬前已获得上万
名⽤⼾付费订阅，⼀直是⽤⼾评价最⾼和回看复习最多的课程。
每次 Alpha 闭⻔分享也特别安排了 30 分钟的问答互动环节，参加的会员朋友可以针对⾃⼰最关⼼
的话题，获得嘉宾的答疑解惑。对闭⻔分享活动感兴趣的朋友，可以点击下⽅活动海报报名加

⼊ Alpha 线上闭⻔私享会会员，⼀起来听听他对市场的最新洞察。

助⼒个⼈关键决策。

Document 260: Compelling risk-reward in US 10-year bonds

Source PDF: all report pdf/Compelling risk-reward in US 10-year bonds.pdf
Report source: 渣打銀行 (Market Watch)
Date: 2026-09-28
Pages: 8
Status: ok

### Page 1

Compelling risk-reward in 
US 10-year bonds  
Summary 
Event: The US 10 -year government bond yield has surged 
past 5.0% to a 19-year high and has now entered our three -
month forecast range of 5.0-5.25%. At the time of writing, the 
10-year yield is trading at 5.2%, its highest since June 2007’s 
high of 5.32%. The next resistance levels are the 2002 high 
of 5.47% and the 2001 high of 5.53%.  
We view the risk/reward for the US 10-year government 
bond after the recent yield spike as  compelling and we 
are initiating a long opportunistic idea at the current 
yield. As the table on the right shows, if the 10 -year yield 
rises to 6%, investors would face less than 1% in negative 
total returns over a one-year period, whereas if the yield falls 
below 5.0%, they would gain at least 6% in total returns.  
While we continue to prefer the 3-7-year bond maturity range 
for income generation (the US 5-year bond yield is also 
above 5%), and broadly prefer corporate over government 
bonds amid solid corporate fundamentals, the US 10 -year 
government bond yield has finally reached reasonable levels 
for investors looking for slightly longer maturities. 
Rationale: We expect the US 10-year government bond yield 
to ease to 4.75-5.0% over a 12-month period due to: 
Easing oil prices and inflation over next 12 months: While 
the yield is likely to remain elevated in the next three months 
due to elevated oil prices caused by geopolitical uncertainty, 
heavy AI investment-driven bond issuance and a resilient US 
economy, it is likely to ease towards the 4.75 -5.0% range 
over 12  months as oil prices moderate either due to a 
resolution in the Middle East or a rerouting of Middle East 
energy supplies and as the impact of tariffs fade.  
We expect s hallower Fed rate hikes than market s: We 
expect a total of 50bps of Fed rate hikes by June 2027, before 
a cut in H2 as US inflation eventually eases towards the Fed’s 
2% target. This  rate trajectory is shallower than c. 95bps of 
further hikes priced by markets over the next 12 months. 
 The benchmark US 10-year government bond yield has 
risen to its highest since 2007, offering medium-term 
investors a compelling risk-reward trade-off 
US 10-year government bond yield and previous highs

Source: Bloomberg, Standard Chartered

At current levels, the US 10-year government bond yield 
offers investors compelling risk-reward trade off

### Page 2

Standard Chartered Bank 
WS Global CIO Office | 28 September 2026

Investor positioning adjustment: In the very near-term, the 
US 10 -year bond yield has room to move lower as hedge 
funds using systematic strategies (CTAs) are extremely short 
US government bonds, raising the chance of short -covering 
if upcoming jobs and inflation data miss estimates. 
A shallow Fed hiking cycle  
We expect the shallowest Fed hiking cycle in almost 
three decades. This should enable the US 10-year yield to 
ease towards 4.75 -5.0% over a 12 -month period. Following 
this month’s increase, we forecast two further 25bp hikes by 
June 2027, followed by a cut in H2 2027. Markets price at 
least three more hikes by June and a strong probability of a 
fourth by September. Our projected 75bp s of total hikes this 
cycle compares with 525bps of hikes in 2022–23, 225bps in 
2015–18, 425bps in 2004–06 and 175bp in 1999–2000. The 
last smaller mid-cycle move was a single 25bps hike in 1997. 
Why we expect a more cautious Fed 
The latest hike restored the Fed’s credibility after renewed 
oil-price pressures, elevated inflation and a balanced labour 
market prompted a unanimous policy vote. However, the 
following factors argue against aggressive tightening: 
Supply-driven inflation: Energy costs and AI-infrastructure 
constraints, rather than excess demand, are driving prices. 
Higher rates cannot repair supply and may weaken growth. 
Labour is no longer the main inflation source:  Labour 
supply and demand are better balanced, wage growth has 
moderated and unemployment is near full employment. AI 
further weakens the traditional Phillips Curve relationship 
between unemployment and inflation. 
Disinflation should resume:  Energy pressures may fade 
after the US mid-term elections, while the one-off inflationary 
effects of tariffs should diminish after 2025–26. 
AI raises the neutral rate , but dampens inflation:  
Investment and productivity may lift the neutral rate ( r*), the 
rate consistent with stable inflation and full employment, 
while efficiency gains limit wage-price spirals. 
Markets are already tightening  financial conditions : 
Higher bond yields have raised borrowing costs and 
restrained activity, doing part of the Fed’s work. 
The cycle began from a higher base:  The Fed funds rate 
started at 3.75%, versus 0.25% in the previous two cycles 
and 1% before that, reducing additional tightening required. 
Monetary transmission has weakened:  Wealthier 
households drive more spending but are less rate -sensitive, 
while lower -income consumers are already constrained. 
Further hikes may , therefore, curb demand less effectively 
while causing disproportionate harm to low-income families. 
 We expect a shallower Fed rate hiking cycle compared 
with market expectations

### Page 3

Standard Chartered Bank 
WS Global CIO Office | 28 September 2026

Small business conditions are deteriorating:  Higher 
financing and energy costs are weakening sentiment, as 
reflected in the latest Beige Book . The renewed 
underperformance of US small capitalisation (S&P 600) and 
mid capitalisation (S&P 400) equity indices  versus the large 
cap S&P 500 index over the past couple of months also show 
rising pressure on small businesses. 
Fiscal and financial -stability risks are rising:  Aggressive 
Fed tightening would increase US debt-servicing costs as the 
US Treasury seeks cheaper funding through shorter-maturity 
issuance, while potentially amplifying financial-system stress. 
Upside risk to bond yields 
Our view of lower bond yields carries the following risks: 
Persistent inflation:  Repeated supply shocks, strong AI -
related investment demand and tighter immigration controls 
could keep inflation above target and force steeper Fed rate 
hikes. With unemployment near the Fed’s estimate of full 
employment, a sharp drop in labour supply could intensify 
wage pressures and destabilise inflation expectations . We 
note that long-term expectations remain contained for now. 
Geopolitical escalation:  An intensification of geopolitical 
conflicts after the mid -term elections could lift energy prices 
and inflation. However, a Democrat -controlled or divided 
Congress may constrain US foreign-policy initiatives through 
tighter budgets, while Republicans could turn less supportive 
as attention shifts to the 2028 presidential race. 
Higher neutral rate: AI investments could raise the neutral 
rate (r*), turning current monetary policy settings 
accommodative. This could necessitate further rate hikes. 
However, the Fed has barely raised its nominal neutral rate 
estimate, lifting it to 3.2% in September from 3.0% a year ago. 
Bottomline: The outlook for rates remains geopolitics 
dependent, with all Fed meetings live . However, based on 
our core scenario, we see the US 10-year yield settling lower 
in the 4.75-5.0% in 6-12 months. A shallow Fed hiking cycle 
due to expected disinflation offers investors a reasonably 
attractive risk-reward in US 10-year government bonds. 
US 30-year government bonds , on the other hand, may 
continue to see elevated yields as the US fiscal deficit is 
unlikely to have improved next year, and may look much 
worse. As a result, “term premium” may keep the ultra -long 
end elevated. Short of some unorthodox policy to artifici ally 
cap them lower, the 30-year yield is likely to continue to drift 
higher towards the 5.5-5.75% range into H2 2027. 
— Jonathan Liang, CIO for Fixed Income and FX   
Rajat Bhattacharya, Senior Investment Strategist 
Cedric Lam, Senior Investment Strategist

tariff effects on inflation to fade over 12 months, 
offsetting AI-driven effects on bond yields

Document 276: EcoNews of 12 October 2026 (2026-10-12)

Source PDF: all report pdf/EcoNews of 12 October 2026 (2026-10-12).pdf
Report source: BNP Paribas
Date: 2026-10-12
Pages: 2
Status: ok

### Page 1

The bank
for a changing
world
5
economic-research.bnpparibas.comEcoWeek 26.37 / 12 October 2026ECONEWS
Find out more in our scenario and forecasts
ADVANCED ECONOMIES
EUROPEAN UNION
Stronger trade posture pays off. The EU and China have agreed to
restrain hybrid cars exports to the EU to a level half or less than cur -
rently projected over the next four years, cutting exports by “several
million units” though still allowing them to rise. China also agreed to

drop its tariffs on EUR 4 bn of EU exports and provide easier access to
Chinese rare earths and magnets under a fast-track licensing sche -
me. These announcements were made at the conclusion of EU Trade
Commissioner Maroš Šefcovic’s visit to Beijing, following a recent es -
calation in trade tensions. Meanwhile, France and Germany released
a joint non-paper on Strengthening European resilience, addressing
global macroeconomic imbalances and market distorting practices

calling notably for:
i) Broadening the tools the European Commission may use to address
unfair trade practices (including undervalued exchange rates) or im -
port surges;
ii) Procedural changes to make them easier to use (qualified majority
of member states needed to block rather than authorise them). These
measures will be discussed during the next EU council on October
15-16th.
The European Commission also unveiled a new critical materials pac -

kage involving 46 strategic extraction, processing or recycling projects
spread across 16 member states (including 5 in France). The goal is to
reduce the EU’s dependence on China for critical metals.
EUROZONE
Price pressures are broadening, activity holds up. Producer price in -
flation accelerated further in August (8.3% y/y 1 from 5.8%), driven by
energy. Pressures are becoming more widespread, notably in capital
goods and above all intermediate goods (6.8%). They are partly offset

by non-durable consumer goods, which remain in deflation (-0.8%).
Given the PPI's close correlation with the HICP , this points to further
upside risks to consumer inflation. On the activity front, final September
PMIs confirmed the flash estimates, with the composite index still solid
at 53.1 and the manufacturing index stable at 52.7. The construction
PMI improved but remains depressed (43.4; +0.4 pt). Retail sales edged
up 0.1% m/m2 in August.

France
Modest spread relief as budget positioning starts. The spread between
French and German 10Y yields narrowed to 136 bps 3 on October 9
(mostly thanks to a decline in the French yield). As the Finance Com -
mittee of the National Assembly started discussing the 2027 budget,
the government reiterated its strong intent not to deviate from the
5% of GDP deficit target and to get a budget adopted in time, likely
without a vote. This is the most likely outcome — but not to be im -

plemented before 10 December (given mandatory timelines) — which
effectively removes any incentive for MPs to back unpopular measures
during the process. We can therefore expect heated debates (including
multiple no-confidence votes being tabled but rejected) and a lack in
fiscal discipline. In parallel, the "Rassemblement National" party ("Na-
tional Rally" in English) presented its fiscal strategy, which includes an
alternative budget for 2027 and an aggressive fiscal consolidation path

throughout 2032. The party called for a referendum to constitutionalise
1 Year-on-year
2 Month-on-month
3 Basis points
a “golden rule” that would require a deficit at least 0.5 pp below the
debt-stabilising threshold (roughly 3% of GDP), as long as the public
debt ratio exceeds 60% of GDP . The golden rule would require a budget
balance adjustment of 0.5 pp per year if the fiscal deficit is more than
1 pp above the stabilising threshold. The adjustment measures put

forward lack specifics but likely overstate savings, understate costs
and rely on overly optimistic growth assumptions (despite large fiscal
consolidation, at least 1.7% annually over the next three years — a
pace reached only 4 times in France since 2008, apart from post-pan-
demic reopening, and well in excess of the roughly 1% growth potential
estimated by most international institutions). That said, it is significant
that this party has chosen to fly the flag for fiscal discipline loud and

clear. Other parties should do the same.
Activity is holding up. New vehicle registrations rose 11% y/y in Sep -
tember, confirming a marked acceleration over the past three months
(+9.4% y/y in Q3). In Q3, growth came entirely from electric vehicles
(+112% y/y), whose share of registrations reached an all-time high
(38% in Q3, 41% in September). Export growth continued in August,
both in services (+5.2% y/y YTD) and in goods (+5.6% y/y YTD) — though

driven by prices rather than volume in the latter case. Manufacturing
output posted only a moderate rebound in August (+0.3% m/m; fol -
lowing cumulative declines of 2.8% over the previous three months),
limited by renewed supply constraints in Q3 (which limited produc -
tion at 31.5% of companies; +5 pp compared to H1). Industrial produc-
tion fell (-0.3% m/m) as a result of a decline in electricity production
(-2.4% m/m) driven by the heatwave. Credit growth remains dynamic.

Bank and market financing to NFCs saw steady growth in August (+4%).
New housing loans rebounded slightly in August (EUR 12.2 bn) fol -
lowing a relatively weak July, whilst interest rates continued to rise.
Consumer credit remained buoyant.
Upcoming: Banque de France monthly Business Survey (Monday), Bu-
siness creations in September (Friday).
Germany
Bullish official growth forecast upgrade despite mixed data. The
German government raised its 2026 GDP growth forecast to 1.3%

— the fastest pace since 2017 — from a prior estimate of 0.5%, citing
stronger-than-expected exports and a surge in government spending.
The 2027 forecast was also raised to 1.1% from 0.9%. Industrial pro -
duction was stronger than expected in August (+2.3% y/y versus -1.7%
in July; the highest level since March 2025 and higher than antici -
pated), driven mainly by strong growth in construction output, prima -
rily in public works (+10.5%; to the highest level since November 2021).

By contrast, manufacturing output fell by -0.2% (after +3.3% in July)
due to a decline in transport equipment production (lowest level since
April 2022). Likewise, new factory orders hit their lowest level in one
year in August (-10.6% m/m). However, this decline is mainly due
to a base effect. Export growth remains solid: +4.4% YTD, despite a
-0.8% m/m decline in August. Exports to the Eurozone continue to rise
(+6.3% y/y YTD), while exports to the U.S. have returned to positive

territory (+1.1%) and those to China continue to decline (-12.9%). The
trade surplus reached EUR 19.5 bn in August (+4% y/y YTD, despite a
-9.8% m/m decline).

### Page 2

6
economic-research.bnpparibas.comEcoWeek 26.37 / 12 October 2026

for a changing

ECONEWS Find out more in our scenario and forecasts
UNITED STATES
Mild slowdown in services and more rate hikes signaled. The non-ma-
nufacturing ISM survey fell 0.5 pp4 to 54.9 in September due to a sharp
slowdown in the business activity component (down 5.2 pp to 56.5 pp,
still a robust expansion). Employment rose and supply pressures in -
tensified for the first time since June, while the price gauge reached

its highest level (74, up 1.4 pp) since 2022. Meanwhile, the minutes
of the September FOMC meeting confirmed the Fed’s hawkish outlook
and noted that the AI-buildout carried growing weight in the reading of
current inflation data and outlook. Governor Waller and St. Louis Fed
President Musalem mentioned the need for additional rate hikes while
remaining flexible on timing. The New York Fed survey of consumer in-
flation expectations showed them rising to 3.9%. We continue to expect

two more hikes, the next one coming in December.
Upcoming: September CPI (Wednesday), September PPI and Retail
Sales (Thursday), September Industrial Production (Friday).
JAPAN
Solid wage growth fails to lift household spending. Total wage growth
eased to 3.8% y/y in August (down 0.5 pp) as bonus payments stalled
(0% y/y, after 5.3% y/y in July). This allowed continued real wage growth
at 1.5% y/y, extending an uninterrupted run of positive readings in 2026

as administrative measures kept inflation in check. However, this did
not prevent real household spending from contracting in August for the
ninth consecutive month (-3.1% y/y).
EMERGING MARKETS
The IIF reports that nonresident portfolio flows to EMs turned negative
at -USD 26.3 bn in September, after USD 11.4 bn in August, the first net
outflow since June, as a hawkish Fed lifted US real yields and the USD.
Equity outflows reached -USD 19.2 bn, led by heavy foreign selling of

Korean equities, while debt flows turned negative at -USD 7.0 bn as
hard currency demand faded and EM credit spreads widened late in
the month. Further hikes from DM central banks will create additional
headwinds, but EM should remain resilient as long as global growth
persists.
ASIA
China
Yuan policy defence. Coinciding with the Beijing visit of the EU trade
commissioner, the People's Bank of China issued an unusually lengthy
formal statement rejecting claims that the yuan is undervalued and

defending its exchange-rate management by reiterating that it has a
‘floating exchange rate regime’ with reference to a basket of currencies.
It further argued that China's trade surpluses are driven by rising in -
dustrial competitiveness rather than currency under-devaluation and
that exchange-rate adjustments will not resolve structural global im -
balances. Nonetheless, it allowed the yuan to hit a four-year high vs.
the CFETS basket — bringing its year-to-date gain to 5.7%. The PBOC

set the USD/CNY's reference rate at 6.7330, the strongest level since
2023.
India
Monetary tightening. The RBI raised its repo rate by 25bp to 5.5%, its
first hike since February 2023, and shifted to a “calibrated tightening”
stance amidst rising inflationary pressures. The RBI introduced new
measures to stabilise the rupee, including direct dollar supplies to
state-owned oil companies and tighter restrictions on FX derivatives.
4 Percentage points

EUROPE, MIDDLE EAST, AFRICA
Central Europe
Cautious monetary policy stance amid mild inflation uptick. Poland’s
and Romania’s Central banks kept policy rates unchanged at 3.75% and
6.5%, respectively. Inflation accelerated in September, with a mild up-
tick in the Czech Republic and Hungary (respectively 2.5% and 1.6% y/y)
and a more entrenched increase in Poland (4% y/y). Inflation is now
above the official target in the Czech Rep. and Poland, but fuel price

caps in both countries should contain inflation dynamics.
Gulf
PMIs for September point for a firm but uneven non-oil recovery. Both
Saudi and UAE PMIs stood at 55.3, driven mostly by export orders in
the UAE and by domestic demand in Saudi Arabia. On the contrary,
Qatar’s PMI fell again (to 46.7), while Kuwait’s PMI also declined but
remained in expansionary zone (at 52.4).
Türkiye
Manufacturing production contracted by 1.6% m/m in August, the lar-

gest monthly decrease since the beginning of the year. On a y/y ba -
sis, it fell by 2.3%. Both domestic demand and exports seem to have
contributed negatively to this poor outcome. On a positive note, in
September, CPI inflation (1.9% m/m, 27.9% y/y) slowed more than the
market expected, and exports rebounded.
LATIN AMERICA
Inflationary pressures slightly up across the region. In Brazil, headline
CPI inflation accelerated to 4.6% y/y from 4.2% y/y in August (above the

central bank's 4.5% target), partly reflecting the reversal of a tempora-
ry electricity price discount in August. In Mexico, inflation rose to 3.5%
in September from 3.3% in August, while in Colombia it reached 6.3%,
slightly up from 6.2% in August. Inflation remained high in Chile but
was stable at 4.1%. In Peru, it was also slightly up (4.2% vs. 4.1%); the
central bank kept its policy rate unchanged at 4.25% for the thirteenth
consecutive month but maintained an upward bias amid rising inflation

and energy prices.
COMMODITIES
Energy: Limited relief. Continuation of skirmishes in the Gulf of Hormuz
and escalation of the conflict between the Houthis and a Saudi-led
coalition left oil prices fluctuating around $100/b for Brent. However,
the complex security situation has been driving the cost of shipping to
record high levels and the price of physical barrel to early April levels.
Late on Friday, President Trump announced an agreement with Russian

President Putin to release to global markets 4.8 million tonnes of die -
sel (roughly 8 days of European consumption), provided conditions in
refineries allow for it, of which 300,000 tonnes immediately. The White
House also announced a suspension of sanctions on Russia’s exports
of diesel until April 2027. Energy experts expect a de minimis price
impact.
European gas prices (TTF) rose by 11.5% over the week to 82 €/MWh.
EU gas inventories reached 73.6% of full capacities on October 11 th

(the official target is 80% at end October).
Food inflation rose sharply in September. The FAO’s Food Price Index
grew by 5.8% y/y, up from just 3% in August. This was driven by cereal
(+17% y/y) and sugar prices (+15%).

economic-research.bnpparibas.comEcoWeek 26.38 / 19 October 2026ECONEWS

IMF ANNUAL MEETINGS
Resilience, but no relief. The IMF's latest global growth forecast is un-
changed from a year ago and from April 2026 (at 3.1% in 2026 and 3.4%
in 2027), despite the energy shock, however with a different geogra -
phical breakdown: many energy importers and war-exposed countries
downgraded, and AI-exposed countries upgraded. Resilience was the

buzzword of the meetings, however it appeared mostly in the rear -
view mirror. Looking ahead, buffers are running thin, fiscal policy needs
urgent consolidation, and downside risks (from energy and AI exube -
rance) loom large. Global headline inflation is expected to rise to 4.7%
in 2026, mainly due to energy and food, before easing back to 4.1% in
2027 (revised +0.2pp). Growth in advanced economies is revised up to
2.1% in 2026 (+0.4pp, driven by Germany), staying at 1.8% in 2027. Eu-

rozone growth is upgraded to 1.1% in 2026 (+0.2pp) and 1.2% in 2027.
US growth stays projected at 2.3% in 2026 and 2027 (+0.1pp). Germany
sees the largest G7 upgrade, to 1.3% in 2026 (+0.6pp) and 1.1% in 2027
(+0.1pp). France is the only major advanced economy downgraded, to
0.4% in 2026 (-0.2pp) and 0.7% in 2027 (-0.2pp). For more detailed
takeaways see Substack and this week’s EcoWeek editorial.
ADVANCED ECONOMIES
EUROPEAN UNION
Trade policy, competitiveness and budget agreements by year-end.

EU leaders asked the Commission to propose by year-end an instru -
ment allowing, in extreme cases, full closure of the single market to
a rival deemed to be attacking the EU economy. It is designed as a
“second-strike” deterrent against unfair practices and retaliation.
The Commission is also exploring extending the hybrid-vehicle safe -
guards approach agreed with China last week to other sectors such
as chemicals and machinery. On the "Made in Europe" label, vehicles

would need to be assembled in Europe with at least 70% of compo -
nent value (excluding batteries) sourced locally and with substantial
R&D in the EU. The UK would be included, provided London aligns with
EU trade defence measures. EU Council also called for decisive pro -
gress on the competitiveness agenda, with the single-market (inclu -
ding 26th regime) and simplification files to be wrapped up by year-end.
On the multi-annual financial framework, the objective remains a deal

by end-2026, so that legislation can be adopted in 2027.
EURO AREA
Moderate tightening does it. Multiple ECB Governing Council members,
speaking from the IMF Meetings in Bangkok, re-underwrote their
“moderate response” approach to the ongoing energy-driven infla -
tion surge. Accordingly, they signaled that additional rate action was
likely but not imminent, acknowledging that the rise in bond yields
was doing part of the job, and that there were no signs yet of second

round effects. Strengthening the case for moderation, the Eurozone in-
dustrial production fell in August for the second month running, by
0.5% m/m, pulled down by pharmaceutical output and motor vehicles.
That said, production in Netherlands surged to a new high (2.5% m/m
and 8.7% y/y), driven by AI machinery and equipment.
France
Business starts booming, insolvencies are stable, growth outlook is
downgraded. Business creations rose by 16% y/y in the 3 rd quarter

(+23% y/y in September), driven mainly by the digital sector and retail.
In parallel, business bankruptcies stabilised in the 3rd quarter according
to Altares, with a 0.5% increase y/y (compared with a 5.4% increase y/y
in Q2), and their impact on employment declined: 50,510 jobs at risk
in Q3, down 3% y/y (see our recent analysis). The Banque de France
halved its estimate for Q3 GDP to 0.1% q/q (same as Insee) due to
the manufacturing sector. Its monthly survey showed a sharp jump in

business uncertainty in September, driven by fuel prices and economic
deterioration (the most acute readings in industry and services since
April). The OAT-bund spread was rangebound (137-142).
Germany
Producer prices are rising. Wholesale prices rose 8.7% y/y (vs. 6.8% in
August) due to higher wholesale prices for energy products and inter -
mediate goods, particularly metals.
UNITED KINGDOM
Summer outperformance and kudos from IMF. Monthly GDP defied

expectations in August and posted a 0.1% m/m against a -0.1% consen-
sus. This marked a slowdown from July (0.4% m/m), but also a fourth
consecutive month of expansion. Industrial production underpinned
the advance: it accelerated to 0.8% m/m (consensus: -0.1%, prior: 0.3%)
thanks to pharmaceuticals. The IMF sees growth accelerating to 1.3%
in 2026 (+0.3 pp since July) and 1.2% in 2027. It also singled out the UK
among advanced economies for its efforts and progress in putting its

fiscal house in order.
UNITED STATES
Inflation heats up, manufacturing accelerates, but an October hike
is unlikely. CPI inflation exceeded expectations at 0.65% m/m in Sep -
tember (up 0.2pp), the fastest monthly pace since April. Accordingly,
the y/y rate climbed to 3.7% (up 0.3pp). The biggest upside surprise,
however, came from the core CPI: at 0.33% m/m (consensus: 0.2%, prior:
0.3%) and 2.6% y/y (consensus: 2.5%, prior: 2.4%); as services remained

sticky while goods accelerated to 0.3% m/m on autos and AI-related
items. Producer prices too rose well above expectations to 1.3% m/m
(consensus: 0.6%, prior: 0.5%) – the largest monthly increase since
March 2022 – and 6.3% y/y (consensus: 5.3%, prior: 5.6%). Industrial
production accelerated in September to 0.7% m/m (up 0.6pp) thanks
to utilities. Manufacturing returned to growth (0.3% m/m, up 0.5pp)
with gains broadening beyond the AI-related and aerospace sectors.

Retail sales excl. autos and gasoline, by contrast, slowed sharply to
0.2% m/m (down 0.9pp). During his speech at the IMF Meetings after
the hotter than expected inflation prints, Chair Warsh declined to hint
at an imminent rate hike. We continue to expect the next rate hike to
occur in December.
To watch this week:
- Developed markets Flash PMIs for October (Friday).
- Eurozone Germany September PPI, October ZEW (Tuesday), France Insee Business Climate (Thursday).

- UK August Employment data (Tuesday), September CPI and PPI (Wednesday), September Retail Sales (Friday).
- Japan September CPI, October PMI (Friday).

JAPAN
Producer price inflation eases. Producer prices fell for a second month
in a row in September, down a further 0.5% m/m, while the yearly rate
slowed for the first time since February (-0.8pp), though it remains
firm at 6.8%. The IMF forecasts growth at 0.7% in both 2026 and 2027,
broadly unchanged. The yen weakened past 158 per dollar again after

reports that the BoJ is leaning against a back-to-back October rate hike.
EMERGING ECONOMIES
Emerging markets
Asia’s outlook upgraded, other regions downgraded.
The IMF upgraded its Asia growth forecast to 4.8% in 2026 and 4.4% in
2027. The largest upgrades are for economies well integrated into the
AI supply chain, notably Taiwan (+5.5pp to 10.7%), South Korea (+0.7pp
to 3.3%), Singapore, Vietnam, Malaysia and to some extent Thailand.
India is forecast to grow 7% in both 2026 and 2027 (fiscal year basis),

up 0.6 and 0.3pp from July on robust domestic demand.
Central Europe’s GDP growth is projected at 2.1% in 2026, slightly
lower than April’s forecast. In 2027, it remains unchanged at 2.3%.
Growth paths are diverse within the region.
In the Middle East & North Africa, GDP is expected to contract by 2%
in 2026. GCC real GDP should fall by 4.8% with only Oman expected
to post positive growth (4.1%). A marked rebound is expected for 2027
(MENA: 7%; GCC: 9.1%).

The growth outlook for Latin America remains subdued, revised down
to 2.3% in 2026 (from 2.4%) and 2.5% in 2027 (from 2.7%). However, in
its "International Trade Outlook 2026" published on 15 October, the
Economic Commission for Latin America and the Caribbean (ECLAC) ex-
pects the region to experience a trade boom in 2026: goods exports are
projected to surge by 20% in value terms (11% from price increases and
9% from volume growth), with trade to Asia (excluding China) soaring

by 32%, while intraregional trade growth lags behind at 8%. Yet, these
export gains are concentrated in commodities and low-value-added
manufacturing, limiting their spillover to broader growth. Stronger
trade is thus providing only a modest boost to the economy.
China
Explosive export growth, subdued inflation and credit growth. In Sep-
tember, export value growth surged to 36.3% y/y from 25% in August,
driven by semiconductors, computer equipment and other AI goods, as

well as green technologies. Export prices surged, especially for semi -
conductors, while total export volumes remained robust. Import value
growth also accelerated to 31% y/y from 28.2% in August, boosted by
both rising volume and rapidly increasing prices. The trade surplus
hit a new monthly record of USD 134 bn in September. CPI inflation
edged up to 0.9% y/y in September, mainly because of a 14.9% rise in
vehicle fuel prices. Food prices continued to fall (-1.7% y/y) and core

inflation was stable at 1%. Producer price inflation accelerated to 5%
(the highest since 2022) due to rising commodity prices. Growth in to-
tal domestic credit outstanding (“social financing”) continued to slow
down, reaching a record low of 7.1% y/y in September. Bank loans to
households declined (-2% y/y) for the seventh consecutive month.
In Q3 2026, real GDP growth rebounded to 4.5% y/y (1.2% q/q) from
4.3% y/y (0.9% q/q), primarily led by industrial production (which rose by

5.1% y/y on average in Q3 vs. 4.6% in Q2). On the demand side, exports
were the key driver while fixed-asset investment continued to contract.
Retail sales posted a small pickup in September but remained flat
in real terms in Q3 as a whole. With growth reaching 4.6% y/y year-to-
date, China needs only a 4.2% growth in Q4 to meet the official target of
4.5%-5% for this year – which seems easily feasible. The IMF trimmed
its growth forecast by 0.1pp to 4.5% in 2026 and 4% in 2027.

Indonesia
MSCI launched a consultation on reclassifying the country from Emer-
ging Market to Standalone Market status, with a final decision due by
end-June 2027, citing opaque shareholding structures and suspected
coordinating trading. Since January 2026, MSCI had frozen Index ad -
justments and threatened only a downgrade to Frontier market status
(like Pakistan), but market participants argued that a Frontier reclas -
sification would not properly reflect the extent of the market’s pro -

blems. Over the medium term, the risk is heavy foreign capital outflows
at a time when net FDI no longer covers the current account deficit.
This leaves Indonesia reliant on foreign portfolio inflows and the ru -
piah exposed to a reversal in sentiment. On the day of this announce -
ment, the Jakarta Composite Index fell by 11.6%, its steepest drop since
January 1998. Among Asian emerging markets, Indonesia has posted
the worst stock market performance since the beginning of the year

(-40.7%).
COMMODITIES
Iran/Oil. More of the same. Brent oil prices have remained broadly
stable this week (0.5% at 107$/b on Monday). Renewed Iranian at -
tacks on tankers in the Strait and intensification of Houthis’ attack over
the Saudi territory feed the geopolitical risk premium on barrel prices.
But IEA announcement of an accelerated 100 mb oil release (above
the March initial target of 400 mb) and reports that the US may lift
sanctions on the Russian and Iranian shadow fleets have contributed to

crude price stability. The spread with physical barrel (Dated Brent) has
declined (6.7$/b on Oct 16th). Trump administration’s various attempts
to lower diesel prices have had a limited impact for now. European gas
prices (TTF) recorded another weekly rise (4.7% w/w to 88.4 €/MWh,
the highest level since Dec 22nd, 2022) on the back of the rising number
of attacks on vessels in the Gulf and persisting disruption in the Strait.
However, EU gas inventories reached 74% of full capacities on October

16th (i.e., almost at the 80% month-end target), which should reduce
support for TTF prices going forward.
y/y = year-on-year
m/m = month-on-month
bp = basis point
pp = percentage point
mb = million barrels

Are investors rational?
The stock market strength and resilience over the last 3 months appears to be a rational response to strong
earnings and only limited signs that higher energy prices are materially affecting broad corporate profits.
Our dbLumina gauge indicates investors have been irrational during market

drops and periods of geopolitical tensions What does it mean to be ‘rational’?
Risk-off periods:
The tariff fears in 2025 and then the Iran
war in 2026 were key buying opportunities
in equity markets. Yet, the overwhelming
fear exhibited by investors in these periods
largely meant they sold off risk assets in an
emotional and irrational response.
Risk-on periods:
It can be rational to buy into a rising
market. Our indicator suggests this is what
happened in Q3 as earnings growth

continued to be strong.
Fear Indicators:
Sharp, disproportionate reactions to
threats, widespread sell-offs on
announcements, panic, flight to safety
beyond what might be fundamentally
warranted.

Luke.Templeman@db.com
How Q3’s emerging themes shape the coming 12 months | October 2026 | Luke Templeman | Luke.Templeman@db.com Deutsche BankResearch Institute
Emerging risks underestimated by the market – part 1
A reallocation of global sovereign reserve assets is accelerating, spurred by
geopolitical uncertainty and the fear of trade and financial sanctions. Notably,
central banks are repatriating their physical assets, such as the Dutch and French

central banks which have recently moved gold reserves out of New York.
Furthermore, a World Gold Council survey reveals that 45% of global central banks
plan to increase their gold reserves over the next 12 months.
What the market underestimates: The market views the recent surges in gold prices
as temporary, cyclical safe-haven flows. In reality, sovereign institutions are
structurally preparing for a more fragmented global trade system where tariff

policies and sanctions may make dollar-clearing assets more difficult.
Impact on key factors in our megatrend model:
Interest rates: At a time when the "world is awash in debt", long-term interest rates
of some countries may be forced structurally higher to attract alternative private
capital.
Inflation: Unexpected dollar depreciation may increase the cost of imported goods
Equity Prices: High-growth US equity valuations, which are highly sensitive to the

long-term risk-free rate, may face multiple compression if bond yields are pushed
higher.
The market currently prices in a material, long-term GDP boost from AI-driven
productivity gains. However, some research from the IMF suggests that over-reliance
on standardised LLMs can produce a high degree of "sameness" and uniformity in
cognitive output. Furthermore, escalating intellectual property and copyright
litigation threaten to bifurcate the quality of AI models based strictly on developers'

licensing budgets.
What the market underestimates: Some investors assume that the exponential
curve of AI hardware capabilities translates directly into exponential economic
output. But there is a risk that if AI tools merely standardise corporate processes
rather than innovate them, and legal barriers restrict access to premium data, the
productivity boom may be less aggressive than expected.
Impact on key factors in our megatrend model:
GDP growth: Structural growth ceiling if AI fails to deliver the multi-percentage

point boost to global productivity required to offset aging demographics.
Equity prices: A downward revaluation of the technology and software sector if
massive AI infrastructure expenditures fail to generate revenue according to existing
expectations.
Geopolitical fragmentation of global reserve assets The AI “sameness“ trap and the productivity stagnation1 2
8

### Page 10

Luke.Templeman@db.com
How Q3’s emerging themes shape the coming 12 months | October 2026 | Luke Templeman | Luke.Templeman@db.com Deutsche BankResearch Institute
Emerging risks underestimated by the market – part 2
Advanced economies are facing a dual demographic crisis: rapidly aging populations
and underfunded pension systems. This is forcing some older people to remain in or
re-enter the workforce out of financial necessity rather than choice.

What the market underestimates: Lower unemployment rates are celebrated as a
sign of economic strength. However, in structurally ageing societies like Japan and
parts of Europe, the growth of the labour force is increasingly driven by part-time,
older workers. Furthermore, G20 nations lack adequate, cohesive policy frameworks
to address these long-term demographic fiscal drains.
Impact on key factors in our megatrend model:
Inflation: An older workforce working out of necessity has less discretionary

spending power, potentially locking economies into persistent, structural demand-
side deflationary forces, offset by supply-side wage pressures in highly skilled
sectors.
GDP growth: Labour productivity growth that is lower than it might otherwise be as
the median age of the workforce climbs, limiting long-term potential GDP.
The geopolitical battleground is focussed on energy blockades (such as the Strait of
Hormuz) but underestimates the weaponisation of critical minerals. China currently

holds ‘escalation dominance’ over rare-earth mineral supply chains. The emerging
risk is the proactive restriction of these minerals to Western firms, such as recent
bans on supplying rare-earth inputs to some US firms.
What the market underestimates: The market treats rare-earth supply constraints as
temporary trade spat negotiation tactics. In reality, as Western nations aggressively
push for reshoring and friend-shoring, China is increasingly using its processing

monopoly as a long-term strategic blockade to try to slow Western high-tech,
defence and the energy transition.
Impact on key factors in our megatrend model:
Inflation: Upwards pressure on inflation as the cost of manufacturing electric
vehicles, semiconductors, and advanced electronics surges due to supply blockades
and the high capital cost of rebuilding localised processing facilities.
Interest rates: Central banks may be forced to keep policy rates higher to combat

the persistent supply-side inflation generated by supply-chain fragmentation.
The fragmentation of the EU’s political centre Critical mineral isolationism and ‘green’ supply chain blockades3 4

### Page 11

Luke.Templeman@db.com
How Q3’s emerging themes shape the coming 12 months | October 2026 | Luke Templeman | Luke.Templeman@db.com Deutsche BankResearch Institute
Emerging themes – part 1
Our dbLumina AI model helps us identify the key emerging themes that have the potential to
most impact markets and economies in the short term
While the previous focus was on the AI hardware squeeze and
commercial competition, the narrative is shifting towards AI's role as a

critical component of national security and geopolitical leverage.
— AI access: We see this in President Trump's framing of AI development as a
geopolitical race ("whoever wins AI wins").
— AI as a bargaining chip: Several examples over the last 12 months including the
2025 deal whereby AMD agreed to pay 15% of sales from certain AI chips to the
US government in exchange for an export license. Even if these deals change, the
trajectory is one where access to AI technology is tied to national economic and

security interests.
— Direct military & security applications: Several examples including, cyberattacks
on critical infrastructure (such as a UK power plant), the use of AI in developing
"exceptional" phages to fight E.coli, and the growing field of AI security
discussions.
Outlook: Expect more government intervention, the use of AI technology access as a
tool in diplomatic negotiations, and a greater focus on the dual-use nature of AI in
both commercial and military contexts. This will likely create regulatory and

operational uncertainty for tech firms.
The trend of reshoring is evolving into a more proactive diplomatic
strategy where nations are forming new, often transactional, alliances
to navigate a world less dominated by a single superpower.
— Proactive alliance building: Canadian Prime Minister Carney has been front and
centre making his first international trip to France and the UK in an effort to
diversify away from traditional reliance on the US. That follows other key

meetings between Japan and India, and more.
— Bypassing traditional powers: Greenland signalled its willingness to turn to
China for investment if Western countries do not provide support, reinforcing the
leverage of some smaller nations.
— Shifting security arrangements: The US pushed for a ‘reassurance force’ in
Ukraine, while French President Macron hosted a "coalition of the willing" to
discuss Europe's defence without US guarantees. Further examples include the

discussion of a Franco-German "nuclear sharing" arrangement.
Outlook: The assumptions of a bipolar world order is giving way to a more complex,
multi-nodal system. This will create greater emphasis on regional blocs and issue-
specific coalitions. The stability of traditional alliances like NATO and the G7 remain
deeply uncertain.
AI’s impact on geopolitics and national security The fracturing of multilateralism and the rise of "Middle Power Countries" 1 2

Luke.Templeman@db.com

Emerging themes – part 2
These four key themes dbLumina identifies are sometimes ‘known’ by investors, however, they may be underestimated by the market,
particularly when there are some potentially series longer-term impacts
Previous talk of “sovereign fiscal fragility” has now manifested itself

into a severe market reaction. Although fears around government debt
and deficits pop up from time to time, the magnitude of September’s
bond sell-off has been compounded by oil price concerns
— Accelerating sovereign sell-off: Concerns about inflation and debt sustainability
are not new but the peaks in bond yields are. Yields on 10-year Treasuries, bunds,
and gilts all rose sharply, with bunds hitting their highest level since 2009.
— Fiscal policy as a direct market catalyst: The market is increasingly sensitive to

fiscal policy announcements. For example, in the UK postponed tax rises, led to a
spike in gilt yields. Similarly, France's political turmoil and concerns over its
budget deficit caused the Franco-German yield spread to widen significantly.
— The “awash in debt" narrative: Treasury Secretary Bessent's comment at the G20
that "The world is awash in debt" signalled that the issue is increasingly top-of-
mind for policymakers.
Outlook: Markets will be increasingly quick to punish any perceived fiscal

irresponsibility. Expect higher borrowing costs, currency volatility, and increased risk
of financial instability, especially for countries with high debt levels.
The strain in the US consumer and labour markets is now diverging
sharply from the health of large corporations, reinforcing the K-shaped
economy.
— Strong corporate performance: Earnings season for Q2 showed remarkably solid
global growth, and the resilience of large corporations was a recurring theme

that helped support equity markets despite other headwinds.
— Weak consumer & labour signals: Economic reports have showed some signs of
a weakening labour market, falling consumer confidence, and stress among low-
income Americans. The New York Fed's survey also showed households expect a
worse financial situation.
— Policy and market disconnect: While strong corporate performance depends on
a robust economy, the underlying consumer weakness is concerning. Expect the

market to continue its twitchy reaction to data, as investors struggle to reconcile
these conflicting signals.
Outlook: This theme is potentially the most consequential for the domestic US
economy. If corporate profits and equity markets "detach" from the real economy as
consumers perceive it, it could lead to significant social and political instability,
making economic policy even more difficult to navigate.
The return of global bond vigilantes The bifurcation of economies: corporate resilience vs. consumer strain3 4

Despite risk, agricultural export prices to remain elevated.
• Ongoing Middle East tensions are increasing volatility and raising energy, input
and shipping costs, while broadly supporting prices for agricultural products.

Impacts vary depending on idiosyncratic supply and demand conditions.
• Risks are exacerbated by the impact of a now confirmed super-sized El Niño,
which could slice off up to 1ppt of New Zealand’s GDP should on-farm
production of both milk and meat contract significantly.
• We have revised up our farmgate milk price forecast for this season by 25c to
$9.75/kg, with milk powder prices holding up better than we expected. El Niño
presents a clear upside risk to this, as it does not appear to have been reflected

in GDT auction prices to date.
• Meanwhile average New Zealand beef export prices should remain elevated over
the coming year, as lean trimming prices reverse recent declines. Sheepmeat
prices are set to ease on rising Australian supply, but remain at elevated levels.
• Kiwifruit and apple export prices are expected to remain elevated, supported
by strong demand for premium varieties, although increasing supply is likely to
keep prices largely flat rather than drive further gains.

• Export prices for New Zealand logs are set to remain broadly stable to slightly
firmer, supported by lower log supply and balanced Chinese inventories.

Source: Macrobond, Stats NZ, Westpac
%yr Index
La Niña
El Niño
Farming is well placed to navigate impacts of a strong El Niño.
• Scientists have now confirmed that the current El Niño event will be the

strongest on record. Temperatures in the Pacific Ocean are now 3.05C above the
average, exceeding the record set in 2015.
• A supersized El Niño increases the probability of drier weather in eastern regions
of New Zealand in coming months, with wetter conditions likelier in the west and
southern areas of the country.
• The potential impact on GDP could be significant. Our previous research has
shown a severe drought could slice off as much as 1ppt from economic growth.

• Much of that is will reflect lower agriculture production, particularly in dairy,
sheep and beef farms. Increased on-farm costs and processing disruptions are
also likely.
• A 1% loss in GDP is likely an upper end estimate of the El Niño impact. A key
consideration is soil moisture levels are currently high, implying some resilience
to future drier conditions.
• Farming practices have become more resilient to prolonged dry periods.
Irrigation is more widely used than in past dry periods and snowmelt levels

appear robust, which should be of particular benefit to Canterbury farms.

Conflict-related disruptions could push input costs higher than expected.
• Farm input costs are on the rise reflecting two key drivers: fuel and
fertiliser costs.
• Diesel and petrol prices have increased by about 50% compared to the same
period in 2025. While average prices fell in the September quarter, they closed

the quarter at an elevated level as tensions between the US and Iran increased
and broadened to encompass Saudi Arabia and the Yemeni Houthis.
• Fertiliser prices are also significantly higher. While the fertiliser market has
stabilised after a period of extreme volatility earlier this year, prices remain
highly sensitive to developments in the Middle East.
• Nitrogen (urea) prices have fallen substantially from a mid-year spike, with an
pick up in Chinese exports now boosting supply. These prices though remain

vulnerable to supply disruptions.
• By contrast, phosphate and other sulphur-based fertiliser prices are tracking
higher and remain at elevated levels, as supply constraints, trade restrictions
and disruptions to shipping offset the impact of softening demand in some
regions of the world.

forecasts
Farmgate milk price forecast revised up on strong protein demand.
• We have revised up our farmgate milk price forecast for this season by 25c to
$9.75/kg. This is above Fonterra’s forecast midpoint of $9.50/kg (range of $8.50-

10.50), but lower than futures market pricing of just over $10/kg.
• We have also opened our forecast for the 2027/28 season at $9.50/kg. Note that
there is a wide margin of uncertainty around the forecast at this stage, and we
will review as the next season approaches.
• Dairy prices at auction have generally held up better than our expectations,
although the performance has varied across products. Trends in global demand
are shifting away from fats and towards proteins, which has boosted demand

and prices for skim milk powder in particular.
• Our forecasts do not include an assumption about the impact of El Niño, which
presents a clear upside risk to prices and a downside risk to volumes. While the
futures market is pricing a strong lift in milk powder prices over the New Zealand
summer, there has been no apparent urgency to buy at the GDT auction.
Fonterra is forecasting a 2% lift in milk volumes over the rest of this season, on
top of what was already a strong increase last season.

• Global milk production also remains ample, although it has slowed in Europe
in recent months. El Niño is less likely to affect conditions among the major
producers in the Northern Hemisphere.

Average beef export prices
US Beef (Imp 95 CL) prices, 1-month advanced
US$ c/lb US$ c/lb
Source: AgriHQ, Stats NZ
Export beef prices to remain elevated over the next year.
• Beef export prices remain well supported but have softened in recent
months. The weakness has been concentrated in lean beef trimming prices,

which have eased since April as high US prices attracted additional imports,
particularly from South America, while tariff-free quotas temporarily boosted
product availability.
• In contrast, premium beef prices have remained firm, supported by tight global
supplies of high-quality beef, constrained US cattle numbers, and resilient
demand in higher-end retail and foodservice channels.
• We expect that lean trimming prices will reverse recent declines in coming

months as temporary increases in supply begin to fade and underlying
shortages, including the historically small US beef herd, re-emerge.
• In 2027, lean trimming prices should remain elevated but broadly flat as
strong US demand and a small US cattle herd are largely offset by rising global
production. From 2028 onwards, expanding global beef production gradually
eases market conditions, putting pressure on prices.
• Meanwhile, favourable supply/demand fundamentals to support premium beef

prices in 2027, with expanding global production to weigh on prices from 2028.
• Overall, beef export prices are likely to remain high, although both nominal and
real prices are expected to moderate over the medium term.

TWI weighted price
Lamb export prices to remain firm, but off recent highs.
• NZ sheepmeat export prices remain near record highs, supported by
constrained global supply and resilient demand from premium markets in

Europe, the UK and North America. Export values rose strongly through 2025/26
despite only modest growth in export volumes, highlighting the strength of
underlying prices.
• Global supply remains tight due to declining sheep flocks and lower slaughter
numbers in New Zealand, the UK and parts of Europe. However, rising Australian
production and exports are expected to gradually lift global supply and increase
competition in key export markets, limiting further price gains.

• Meanwhile, stronger demand from the UK, EU and North America has improved
export returns and reduced New Zealand's reliance on China, where economic
weakness has made consumers more price sensitive and encouraged a shift
towards cheaper proteins and cuts.
• The outlook remains positive but more balanced. Tight supply should keep
prices above historical averages over the coming year, although rising Australian
exports, softer demand growth, affordability pressures, signs of buyer

resistance and (eventually) a stronger NZ dollar could see prices ease modestly
from current elevated levels into 2027.

Capacity is catching up with demand, limiting further price gains.
• NZ kiwifruit export prices have pushed to record highs, supported by strong
demand in North America, Europe, Japan and Korea, as well as continued

consumer preference for premium varieties such as SunGold.
• Export prices continue to edge higher even as a record crop and larger fruit
volumes are helping to reduce market tightness. Zespri notes increased
competition from summer fruit, pricing pressure in parts of Asia and a larger-
than-expected crop.
• Kiwifruit export prices are set to remain high but mostly flat over the next
year, as strong demand for premium varieties such as SunGold and RubyRed is

increasingly offset by rising production and price pressures in Asia.
• Apple export prices are also at record highs, supported by strong demand in
Asia, North America and India, alongside a continued shift towards higher-value
cultivars and premium varieties.
• These factors should keep export prices elevated over the next 12 months,
supporting grower revenues and profitability.
• However, further gains could be limited. Rising New Zealand production,

recovering supplies from key growing regions and larger global apple crops are
expected to increase supply availability, reducing market tightness and keeping
prices broadly stable.

Source: AgriHQ
Index IndexLog prices look moribund.
• China remains the dominant market for New Zealand logs, with prices highly
sensitive to construction activity, sawmill demand and daily log offtake. In that
regard, weak housing and furniture demand continue to weigh on prices.

• Chinese softwood log inventories are estimated at around 2.2 to 2.5m m³,
equivalent to roughly 40 to 45 days of inventory cover at current offtake rates.
This suggests inventory levels are broadly balanced and exerting little pressure
on log prices.
• At the same time, reduced New Zealand log supply, including lower harvesting
volumes and the wind-down of salvage logging, is providing some support to
prices. Freight costs also remain an important driver of at-wharf-gate returns,

with currently high shipping costs crimping returns to forest owners.
• NZ log export prices are expected to remain broadly stable to modestly firmer
over the next 12 months, supported by lower domestic supply, balanced Chinese
inventory levels and tentative signs of stabilisation in log demand.
• However, any recovery is likely to be gradual, with weakness in China's property
sector, uncertainty around shipping costs and softer global growth continuing to

constrain demand and cap upside potential.

The Week Ahead October 23, 2026

CENTRAL BANK MEETINGS

Bank of Thailand (BOT) (October 28)

We expect the BOT to maintain its policy 
rate at 1.00% at its 28 October meeting, 
extending its pause for a fourth 
consecutive decision as it balances 
inflationary pressures against uneven 
and subdued economic growth. Our view 
is aligned with fixed market income 
pricing. Headline inflation rose anew to 
2.8% yoy in September 2026, driven by 
higher energy, food, and core price 
increases, but remained within the upper 
end of the central bank’s 1 -3% target 
range. As the BOT is still likely to look 
through what it views as temporary, 
supply-driven inflationary pressu res, it 
will likely refrain from tightening policy, 
and keep interest rates accommodative 
to support the soft and uneven growth 
cycle.

European Central Bank (October 29)

We expect the ECB to extend its pause, 
keeping the deposit facility rate at 2.5%. 
Market participants will instead focus on 
the central bank’s assessment of rising 
French and Italian bond yields and 
widening spreads versus Bunds. While 
we do not expect dir ect references to 
individual countries, the ECB is likely to 
signal that recent developments in 
France reflect the country’s weaker 
fiscal and debt profile rather than a 
broader systemic concern. Policymakers 
will seek to differentiate between a 
localised bond sell -off and a Eurozone -
wide market disruption, justifying their 
decision to monitor developments rather 
than implement specific measures at this 
juncture. Meanwhile, inflation continues 
to rise, with little relief expected in 
upcoming readings given elevated 
global fuel prices. 
Bank of Japan (October 30)

The Bank of Japan is expected to keep 
the overnight call rate unchanged at 1.25% 
at this meeting, pausing after the 25bp 
hike in September. The BOJ is likely to 
assess the impact of the previous rate 
hike before taking further action. The 
recent stabilisat ion of the yen also 
reduces the urgency of another hike at 
this meeting.

The tightening bias should remain intact. 
Growth and inflation remain broadly on 
track, leaving room for the BOJ to revise 
up its FY2026 –FY2027 GDP and CPI 
forecasts. Wage growth has also 
remained strong, with Rengo reportedly 
targeting wage increases of 6% in 2027, 
reinforcing the BOJ’s confidence that 
underlying inflation can be sustained 
around its 2% target. We continue to 
expect the policy rate to rise to 1.50% in 
December 2026 or January 2027, 
followed by a further increase to 1.75% 
by 2Q 2027.

FORTHCOMING DATA 
RELEASES

Hong Kong SAR

Export growth is expected to accelerate 
from 53.0% yoy in August to 55.0% in 
September, in line with the pickup in 
China’s export growth from 25.0% to 
36.3%. As a key re-export hub for China,

### Page 3

The Week Ahead October 23, 2026 
                                                                                                                                         3

Hong Kong should benefit from robust 
AI-related electronics trade, with 
electrical machinery, equipment and 
parts exports to Hong Kong accounting 
for 20.6% of China’s total exports. 
Import growth is also expected to remain 
firm, supported by resilient expo rt 
orders and gradually improving 
investment sentiment, sustaining 
demand for intermediate and capital 
goods.

Singapore

Singapore’s industrial production (IP) 
likely remained in expansion for a 13th 
consecutive month in September 2026, 
although we expect growth to moderate 
to 7.6% yoy from August’s strong 15.4% 
yoy pace. IP growth continued to be 
underpinned by the electronics and 
precision engineering clusters, which 
has been key drivers of Singapore’s 
manufacturing strength, reflecting 
sustained demand for artificial 
intelligence-related hardware. However, 
performance across clusters remained 
uneven, with chemicals and biomedical 
manufacturing likely contracting, and 
tempering overall gains.

South Korea

The preliminary estimate for 3Q26 GDP 
is likely to show growth remaining solid 
at 3.2% yoy , slightly below 3.7% in 2Q 
and 3.8% in 1Q. Nominal GDP growth is 
likely to remain in double digits, 
significantly outpacing real GDP growth, 
thanks to improved terms of trade amid 
rising memory export prices. By 
expenditure, exports picked up in 3Q in 
both nominal and real terms, supported 
by stronger semiconductor shipments. 
Investment also appeared robust, as 
reflected in the equipment investment 
estimation index and machinery order 
index. Private consumption, however, 
appeared to weaken amid the sharp 
KOSPI correction since July following  
the introduction of leveraged ETF 
regulations. Our full -year GDP growth 
forecasts of 3.5% for 2026 and 2.4% for 
2027 remain on track.

Taiwan

The preliminary estimate for 3Q GDP is 
likely to show growth remaining strong 
at 12 –13% yoy , close to 12.9% in 2Q, 
albeit below the peak of 15.4% in 1Q. 
Exports maintained strong double -digit 
growth in 3Q (44.6% yoy  in nominal 
terms and 21.1% in real terms), supported 
by robust shipments of semiconductors, 
servers and other AI -related products. 
Machinery investment also sustained 
strong double-digit growth, underpinned 
by higher capacity utilisation in the ICT 
sector and a resulting increase in capital 
goods imports. Private consumption 
appeared to have strengthened further, 
supported by stock market gains, 
improving wage growth, and the 
associated wealth and income effects. 
Construction investment remained the 
weak s pot, weighed down by elevated 
inventories in the new-home market and 
the ongoing need for inventory digestion. 
We maintain our 2026 and 2027 GDP 
growth forecasts at 11.6% and 5.6%, 
respectively, with risks tilted to the 
upside.

United States: Elevated inflation and high growth
02/10/2026 4
August 2026: Marked improvement in services
business confidence, from an already high level
September 2026: Manufacturing business

confidence remains stable at a high level
August 2026: Increasing inflationary pressures September 2026: Supplier deliveries do not get
longer
August 2026: Consumers are a bit more
concerned about their personal finances
August 2026: Headline inflation resists to the
upside
U.S. macroeconomic performance has remained solid since the onset of the shock, as illustrated in particular by Q2 growth
of +3.8% and +9% (annualized quarterly rate) in consumer spending and private nonresidential investment, respectively.

In the manufacturing sector, business sentiment has gained momentum despite the shock, while the impact of this shock
on the services sector has remained very limited. The immediate reaction of consumer confidence was, however, more
negative before beginning to improve, which was nevertheless interrupted in August (latest available data for households’
sentiment on their future personal financial situation). The University of Michigan’s composite indicator continued to

deteriorate in September, due to the sharp rise in fuel prices. But declining confidence still does not translate into reduced
spending—at least not at the aggregate level.
On the inflation front, the spike immediately following the outbreak of the conflict in Iran was more pronounced than in
the months following Russia’s invasion of Ukraine. The decline in June still left inflation at a high level, but its stability in
July and August was notable (3.4% y/y), as was the latest—the third consecutive—slight decline in inflation excluding

energy in August (2.5% y/y, same rate as in February). Given the existing inflationary pressures, however, inflation is highly
likely to resume its upward trend in September.
Source: BLS, ISM, NFIB, University of Michigan, BNP Paribas
